Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, nice to meet you too. Um, so I'm here because... well, honestly, everything is just a giant mess right now. I just feel like I absolutely cannot get my schoolwork and my little accessories shop organized at the same time. It's like, if I focus on one, the other completely falls apart, you know? 

I started this shop—I make these little hair clips and charms and stuff—and it was supposed to be fun, but now... well, my room and my desk are just covered. Literally covered. My crafting supplies, all the little beads and wires and packaging, they get completely mixed up with my receipts from shipping out orders, and then right in the middle of all of that are my assignment notes for my classes. The due dates are just slipping right past me because everything just ends up in these massive piles everywhere. I look at my desk and there's a pile for school, a pile for the shop, a pile of I-don't-even-know-what, and I just stand there and I don't know what to do first. I get completely overwhelmed because there's so much to do and no clear place to start, so I end up just kind of moving the piles around instead of actually getting anything done. It's just really out of hand.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, wow, as a child? Um, well, yes, but it wasn't just the organization, it was kind of just my whole vibe at school, honestly. Like, my mom actually still has all these old report cards and parent-teacher notes from when I was in elementary school. She keeps them in this big plastic storage bin in the hallway closet, which is ironic because she's incredibly organized and I'm obviously, you know, drowning in beads and shipping labels. 

But anyway, she actually pulled some of those notes out recently because we were talking about how stressed I am right now, and it’s so crazy to read them now because every single teacher wrote the exact same things. They were always saying how I was literally always out of my seat. Like, I just could not stay in my chair during reading time or math or whatever, I always had to be up and walking around the classroom. And the notes always mentioned how I was constantly talking before being called on—like I would just blurt out the answers or whatever popped into my head before the teacher even finished asking the question because I just couldn't hold the thought in, I had to say it right then. 

And then with group work, they said I was always rushing through all the group activities. Which is funny because the teachers would even write that I totally understood the work! It wasn't that I was confused or trying to avoid doing the assignment, I just... I understood it, so I wanted to speed through it as fast as humanly possible and move on to the next thing. I just had this constant need to go, go, go, and I’d end up dragging the rest of my group along with me at warp speed. So, yeah, looking back at those notes, I guess I've always been kind of all over the place and rushing around like that.

Um, honestly? Not really? Or at least, I haven't really noticed it being a problem anywhere else. I mean, when I really sit down and think about it, my whole life right now is basically just those two things anyway—school and the shop. I don't have, like, a regular 9-to-5 job or a whole lot of other major responsibilities that require a ton of managing, so there isn't really much else to get disorganized, if that makes sense. 

Like, I guess you could look at my car, but my car is actually pretty fine. I mean, sure, there are maybe a couple of empty iced coffee cups in the cup holders and maybe a sweater I tossed in the back seat last weekend when I went to the movies with my friend Jess—we went to see that new thriller, which was actually terrible, by the way, the plot made absolutely zero sense and we were both just complaining about it the entire drive home—but anyway, the car isn't like a disaster zone. It's just normal lived-in. It's not like my desk where there are literal mountains of things I can't sort through. 

And my closet is... well, it's just normal, you know? I just kind of shove my clothes in there or hang them up really quick. It's definitely not some perfectly color-coded Pinterest closet, but it doesn't stress me out or stop me from getting dressed in the morning. And my bathroom just has my makeup bag on the counter, which is pretty contained. So I don't really have those massive, overwhelming piles of doom anywhere else in my life. It's really just the crafting supplies and the homework that are completely colliding and destroying my space. So, yeah, I really don't think I have that same kind of trouble in other areas. I just don't have that much else going on that even needs organizing, you know?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um, social life? I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by social life. Like, my friends are pretty understanding because they know how I am, but... it's definitely bleeding into other things, and it's actually getting really bad. Like, really, really bad. 

So, with school, because everything is just this massive, swirling vortex on my desk, I completely missed some of the required steps for my program this semester. Like, there were these mandatory advising forms and some sort of progress check-in thing that I was supposed to submit online, and I had the papers printed out—I literally remember printing them out at the library because my printer was out of ink, which is a whole other story because I bought the wrong cartridge twice—but I put them in one of the piles, and then they just vanished into the void. And because I missed those steps, my financial aid is actually at risk right now. I got this terrifying email from the financial aid office with all this red text, and I've been trying to call them, but I keep forgetting their office hours, and honestly, I'm just so panicked about it that I feel sick to my stomach just thinking about it. If I lose my financial aid, I don't even know what I'll do. 

And then with the shop... it's definitely affecting how people see me. I collaborate sometimes with a couple of other girls who make, like, custom resin keychains and tote bags, and we were supposed to do this big joint giveaway on Instagram. We had it all planned out in this group chat. But because my supplies were a disaster and I couldn't find half my packaging materials, and I kept losing track of who was doing what, I ended up totally dropping the ball on my end of the project. I was supposed to mail out my part of the prize bundles to the winners, and it took me like three weeks longer than I promised. And now... well, I've noticed they've started depending on me less. They're planning another big holiday drop right now, and they didn't even ask me if I wanted to be part of it. I saw them talking about it on their stories, and it just really stung, you know? It sucks because I love working with them, but I can't even blame them because I was so unreliable last time. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some serious trouble, and I just feel like I'm letting everyone down, including myself.

It's just normal lived-in. It's not like my desk where there are literal mountains of things I can't sort through. \n\nAnd my closet is... well, it's just normal, you know? I just kind of shove my clothes in there or hang them up really quick. It's definitely not some perfectly color-coded Pinterest closet, but it doesn't stress me out or stop me from getting dressed in the morning. And my bathroom just has my makeup bag on the counter, which is pretty contained. So I don't really have those massive, overwhelming piles of doom anywhere else in my life. It's really just the crafting supplies and the homework that are completely colliding and destroying my space. So, yeah, I really don't think I have that same kind of trouble in other areas. I just don't have that much else going on that even needs organizing, you know?\n\nDoctor: Outside of school and your shop, do these problems ever cause trouble in your relationships or social life?\n\nPatient: Well, um, social life? I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by social life. Like, my friends are pretty understanding because they know how I am, but... it's definitely bleeding into other things, and it's actually getting really bad. Like, really, really bad. \n\nSo, with school, because everything is just this massive, swirling vortex on my desk, I completely missed some of the required steps for my program this semester. Like, there were these mandatory advising forms and some sort of progress check-in thing that I was supposed to submit online, and I had the papers printed out—I literally remember printing them out at the library because my printer was out of ink, which is a whole other story because I bought the wrong cartridge twice—but I put them in one of the piles, and then they just vanished into the void. And because I missed those steps, my financial aid is actually at risk right now. I got this terrifying email from the financial aid office with all this red text, and I've been trying to call them, but I keep forgetting their office hours, and honestly, I'm just so panicked about it that I feel sick to my stomach just thinking about it. If I lose my financial aid, I don't even know what I'll do. \n\nAnd then with the shop... it's definitely affecting how people see me. I collaborate sometimes with a couple of other girls who make, like, custom resin keychains and tote bags, and we were supposed to do this big joint giveaway on Instagram. We had it all planned out in this group chat. But because my supplies were a disaster and I couldn't find half my packaging materials, and I kept losing track of who was doing what, I ended up totally dropping the ball on my end of the project. I was supposed to mail out my part of the prize bundles to the winners, and it took me like three weeks longer than I promised. And now... well, I've noticed they've started depending on me less. They're planning another big holiday drop right now, and they didn't even ask me if I wanted to be part of it. I saw them talking about it on their stories, and it just really stung, you know? It sucks because I love working with them, but I can't even blame them because I was so unreliable last time. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some serious trouble, and I just feel like I'm letting everyone down, including myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid these problems get a lot worse after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been a real problem for about 2 years and 2 months, since I changed schools and had to manage the business program and my accessories shop with less structure.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well, yes, actually. I can pinpoint it pretty exactly, honestly. It's been a really major problem for about two years and two months now. Because that was right around the time I changed schools, and that transition just completely threw my entire life out of whack. 

Like, before that, I feel like things were at least a little bit more structured? But when I transferred and started this new business program, everything just completely shifted. The professors in this program, they do not hold your hand at all, which I guess is what you're supposed to expect, but they literally just hand you a massive syllabus on day one and expect you to manage your entire existence around it. There are no reminders, no daily check-ins, no set schedule for when you should be working on what... it's just entirely up to you. 

And right at that exact same time—about two years and two months ago—I was really trying to ramp up my accessories shop. I was doing more custom orders and expanding my inventory, because I thought, you know, I'm in a business program now, I should really be running my own business more seriously to get that real-world experience. But trying to manage both the business program and the shop at the exact same time, with suddenly way, way less structure in my day-to-day life... it was just a recipe for total disaster. I just didn't have that built-in routine anymore telling me where to be and what to do, so everything just started bleeding together. I'd be up at 2 AM trying to package orders when I should have been studying, and then I'd be sitting in a lecture totally zoning out because I was stressing about shipping labels. So, yeah, it was definitely that specific transition. It's just been two years and two months of absolute chaos since I made that switch and lost all my structure.

Like, really, really bad. \n\nSo, with school, because everything is just this massive, swirling vortex on my desk, I completely missed some of the required steps for my program this semester. Like, there were these mandatory advising forms and some sort of progress check-in thing that I was supposed to submit online, and I had the papers printed out—I literally remember printing them out at the library because my printer was out of ink, which is a whole other story because I bought the wrong cartridge twice—but I put them in one of the piles, and then they just vanished into the void. And because I missed those steps, my financial aid is actually at risk right now. I got this terrifying email from the financial aid office with all this red text, and I've been trying to call them, but I keep forgetting their office hours, and honestly, I'm just so panicked about it that I feel sick to my stomach just thinking about it. If I lose my financial aid, I don't even know what I'll do. \n\nAnd then with the shop... it's definitely affecting how people see me. I collaborate sometimes with a couple of other girls who make, like, custom resin keychains and tote bags, and we were supposed to do this big joint giveaway on Instagram. We had it all planned out in this group chat. But because my supplies were a disaster and I couldn't find half my packaging materials, and I kept losing track of who was doing what, I ended up totally dropping the ball on my end of the project. I was supposed to mail out my part of the prize bundles to the winners, and it took me like three weeks longer than I promised. And now... well, I've noticed they've started depending on me less. They're planning another big holiday drop right now, and they didn't even ask me if I wanted to be part of it. I saw them talking about it on their stories, and it just really stung, you know? It sucks because I love working with them, but I can't even blame them because I was so unreliable last time. Because that was right around the time I changed schools, and that transition just completely threw my entire life out of whack. \n\nLike, before that, I feel like things were at least a little bit more structured? But when I transferred and started this new business program, everything just completely shifted. The professors in this program, they do not hold your hand at all, which I guess is what you're supposed to expect, but they literally just hand you a massive syllabus on day one and expect you to manage your entire existence around it. There are no reminders, no daily check-ins, no set schedule for when you should be working on what... it's just entirely up to you. \n\nAnd right at that exact same time—about two years and two months ago—I was really trying to ramp up my accessories shop. I was doing more custom orders and expanding my inventory, because I thought, you know, I'm in a business program now, I should really be running my own business more seriously to get that real-world experience. But trying to manage both the business program and the shop at the exact same time, with suddenly way, way less structure in my day-to-day life... it was just a recipe for total disaster. I just didn't have that built-in routine anymore telling me where to be and what to do, so everything just started bleeding together. I'd be up at 2 AM trying to package orders when I should have been studying, and then I'd be sitting in a lecture totally zoning out because I was stressing about shipping labels. So, yeah, it was definitely that specific transition. It's just been two years and two months of absolute chaos since I made that switch and lost all my structure.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to solve?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Um, no? Not really. She keeps them in this big plastic storage bin in the hallway closet, which is ironic because she's incredibly organized and I'm obviously, you know, drowning in beads and shipping labels. \n\nBut anyway, she actually pulled some of those notes out recently because we were talking about how stressed I am right now, and it’s so crazy to read them now because every single teacher wrote the exact same things. They were always saying how I was literally always out of my seat. Like, I just could not stay in my chair during reading time or math or whatever, I always had to be up and walking around the classroom. And the notes always mentioned how I was constantly talking before being called on—like I would just blurt out the answers or whatever popped into my head before the teacher even finished asking the question because I just couldn't hold the thought in, I had to say it right then. \n\nAnd then with group work, they said I was always rushing through all the group activities. Which is funny because the teachers would even write that I totally understood the work! It wasn't that I was confused or trying to avoid doing the assignment, I just... I understood it, so I wanted to speed through it as fast as humanly possible and move on to the next thing. I just had this constant need to go, go, go, and I’d end up dragging the rest of my group along with me at warp speed. So, yeah, looking back at those notes, I guess I've always been kind of all over the place and rushing around like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have the same kind of trouble staying organized in other parts of your life besides school and your shop?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly? Not really? Or at least, I haven't really noticed it being a problem anywhere else. I mean, when I really sit down and think about it, my whole life right now is basically just those two things anyway—school and the shop. I don't have, like, a regular 9-to-5 job or a whole lot of other major responsibilities that require a ton of managing, so there isn't really much else to get disorganized, if that makes sense. \n\nLike, I guess you could look at my car, but my car is actually pretty fine. I mean, sure, there are maybe a couple of empty iced coffee cups in the cup holders and maybe a sweater I tossed in the back seat last weekend when I went to the movies with my friend Jess—we went to see that new thriller, which was actually terrible, by the way, the plot made absolutely zero sense and we were both just complaining about it the entire drive home—but anyway, the car isn't like a disaster zone. It's just normal lived-in. It's not like my desk where there are literal mountains of things I can't sort through. \n\nAnd my closet is... well, it's just normal, you know? I just kind of shove my clothes in there or hang them up really quick. It's definitely not some perfectly color-coded Pinterest closet, but it doesn't stress me out or stop me from getting dressed in the morning. And my bathroom just has my makeup bag on the counter, which is pretty contained. So I don't really have those massive, overwhelming piles of doom anywhere else in my life. It's really just the crafting supplies and the homework that are completely colliding and destroying my space. So, yeah, I really don't think I have that same kind of trouble in other areas. I just don't have that much else going on that even needs organizing, you know?\n\nDoctor: Outside of school and your shop, do these problems ever cause trouble in your relationships or social life?\n\nPatient: Well, um, social life? I mean, I guess it depends on what you mean by social life. Like, my friends are pretty understanding because they know how I am, but... it's definitely bleeding into other things, and it's actually getting really bad. Like, really, really bad. \n\nSo, with school, because everything is just this massive, swirling vortex on my desk, I completely missed some of the required steps for my program this semester. Like, there were these mandatory advising forms and some sort of progress check-in thing that I was supposed to submit online, and I had the papers printed out—I literally remember printing them out at the library because my printer was out of ink, which is a whole other story because I bought the wrong cartridge twice—but I put them in one of the piles, and then they just vanished into the void. And because I missed those steps, my financial aid is actually at risk right now. I got this terrifying email from the financial aid office with all this red text, and I've been trying to call them, but I keep forgetting their office hours, and honestly, I'm just so panicked about it that I feel sick to my stomach just thinking about it. If I lose my financial aid, I don't even know what I'll do. \n\nAnd then with the shop... it's definitely affecting how people see me. I collaborate sometimes with a couple of other girls who make, like, custom resin keychains and tote bags, and we were supposed to do this big joint giveaway on Instagram. We had it all planned out in this group chat. But because my supplies were a disaster and I couldn't find half my packaging materials, and I kept losing track of who was doing what, I ended up totally dropping the ball on my end of the project. I was supposed to mail out my part of the prize bundles to the winners, and it took me like three weeks longer than I promised. And now... well, I've noticed they've started depending on me less. They're planning another big holiday drop right now, and they didn't even ask me if I wanted to be part of it. I saw them talking about it on their stories, and it just really stung, you know? It sucks because I love working with them, but I can't even blame them because I was so unreliable last time. So, yeah, it's definitely causing some serious trouble, and I just feel like I'm letting everyone down, including myself.\n\nDoctor: Did these problems get a lot worse after a specific stressful event or major life change?\n\nPatient: Um, well, yes, actually. I can pinpoint it pretty exactly, honestly. It's been a really major problem for about two years and two months now. Because that was right around the time I changed schools, and that transition just completely threw my entire life out of whack. \n\nLike, before that, I feel like things were at least a little bit more structured? But when I transferred and started this new business program, everything just completely shifted. The professors in this program, they do not hold your hand at all, which I guess is what you're supposed to expect, but they literally just hand you a massive syllabus on day one and expect you to manage your entire existence around it. There are no reminders, no daily check-ins, no set schedule for when you should be working on what... it's just entirely up to you. \n\nAnd right at that exact same time—about two years and two months ago—I was really trying to ramp up my accessories shop. I was doing more custom orders and expanding my inventory, because I thought, you know, I'm in a business program now, I should really be running my own business more seriously to get that real-world experience. But trying to manage both the business program and the shop at the exact same time, with suddenly way, way less structure in my day-to-day life... it was just a recipe for total disaster. I just didn't have that built-in routine anymore telling me where to be and what to do, so everything just started bleeding together. I'd be up at 2 AM trying to package orders when I should have been studying, and then I'd be sitting in a lecture totally zoning out because I was stressing about shipping labels. So, yeah, it was definitely that specific transition. It's just been two years and two months of absolute chaos since I made that switch and lost all my structure.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about many different things, even when there isn’t an immediate problem to solve?\n\nPatient: Um, no? Not really. I mean, I definitely worry about the stuff I just told you about, like the financial aid and the shop and letting my friends down, but those are all actual, happening-right-now disasters, you know? Like, there is a very immediate problem to solve there. But just... worrying about random things or hypotheticals? No, I don't really do that. Honestly, I feel like I don't even have the brain capacity to worry about things that aren't actively on fire right in front of me. \n\nLike, my mom is a huge worrier. She'll call me out of nowhere and be like, \"Did you check your tire pressure? What if you get a flat on the highway?\" or \"Are you thinking about what you're going to do for retirement?\" And I'm just like, Mom, I am literally twenty-one, and I am just trying to find my keys so I can get to the post office before it closes. I don't have the luxury of worrying about my tires or the economy or whatever. Or like, my friend Jess—the one I went to that terrible movie with—she's always stressing about, like, what the post-grad job market is going to look like in two years, and she reads all these articles about it and gets herself totally worked up. And I just look at her like she has two heads because I am literally just trying to survive Tuesday. \n\nSo, yeah, if there isn't a glaring, immediate problem staring me in the face, I'm probably not even thinking about it, let alone worrying about it. I'm way too busy trying to keep my head above water with the chaos I've already created to invent new things to stress over. So, no, not really."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\n  \"key\": \"functional_impairment\",\n  \"name\": \"Functional Impairment\",\n  \"description\": \"required\",\n  \"reason\": \"The doctor is asking about the negative impact or consequences of the symptoms on the patient's relationships or social life, which corresponds to functional impairment.\"\n}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
